Customer Success English

A customer success English curriculum for onboarding, adoption, health scoring, escalations, QBRs, renewals, churn risk, difficult customers, product feedback, and expansion conversations.

Words into action

Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For customer success managers, account managers, onboarding specialists, support escalation leads, renewals managers, implementation teams, and post-sale revenue leaders.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Clarify owners, dependencies, and acceptance checks before confirming a customer launch date.
  • Explain usage measures with the right population and distinguish access from meaningful adoption.
  • Communicate confirmed impact, temporary relief, unresolved questions, and the next update under pressure.
  • Present measured change, disclose valuation assumptions, and separate released capacity from proven financial return.
  • Explore changed priorities, verify decision roles, and describe renewal risk without treating it as a verdict.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

01Onboarding and Implementation ExpectationsTurn a hoped-for launch into a shared readiness plan+

Your goal

Clarify owners, dependencies, and acceptance checks before confirming a customer launch date.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Turn a hoped-for launch into a shared readiness plan

Customer lead Omar expects a launch on Friday, 9 October. The export owner and training slot are still unassigned. Customer success manager Lena cannot confirm readiness. The agreed launch conditions are an approved customer export, a successful test import, user acceptance of the main workflow, and completed administrator training. The customer owns data preparation; the supplier owns import mapping and training delivery. No one has yet confirmed that the required people are available. A readiness review is scheduled for Thursday at 15:00 UTC.

Omar: Customer implementation lead / Lena: Customer success manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the status of the Friday launch?

Answer and explanation

A. A target whose readiness conditions are not yet confirmed

The target remains conditional because required tasks and people are not yet confirmed.

2. Who owns preparation of the customer data?

Answer and explanation

A. The customer

The brief assigns data preparation to the customer, separately from supplier import mapping.

3. When is the readiness review?

Answer and explanation

A. Thursday at 15:00 UTC

The stated review has both a day and an explicit time zone.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A target date is not evidence of readiness. The four stated conditions are export approval, a successful test import, workflow acceptance, and administrator training. The Thursday review checks those conditions; holding the meeting alone does not satisfy them.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
onboarding
The process of preparing a customer to use a service effectively.coordinate onboarding
implementation
Work to configure, connect, and introduce a solution.plan the implementation
kickoff
The initial meeting aligning purpose, people, and approach.run a kickoff
success plan
A shared record of outcomes, actions, ownership, and measures.agree a success plan
target date
A desired date whose commitment status must be specified.qualify the target date
go-live
The start of operational use under agreed conditions.confirm go-live readiness
dependency
A task or condition on which another step relies.identify dependencies
prerequisite
Something required before a particular activity can proceed.verify prerequisites
data migration
Moving data between systems with appropriate checks.coordinate data migration
source export
Data extracted from the originating system.approve the source export
data mapping
Specifying how source fields correspond to destination fields.validate data mapping
data owner
The role accountable for the relevant data and its use.confirm the data owner
test import
A controlled trial of bringing data into the destination.complete a test import
UAT
User acceptance testing; users checking the solution against agreed needs.schedule UAT
acceptance criterion
A defined condition used to judge whether work is acceptable.agree acceptance criteria
administrator
A user with specified management permissions in the service.train administrators
training slot
A scheduled period with the required trainer and participants.confirm the training slot
readiness review
A check of whether the conditions for the next stage are met.hold a readiness review
cutover
The coordinated transition into a new operational arrangement.plan the cutover
rollback plan
An approved approach for reversing a change if required.review the rollback plan
hypercare
A defined period of enhanced support after introduction.define hypercare coverage
handover
Transfer of responsibility with the necessary information.complete the handover
RACI
Responsible, accountable, consulted, and informed; a role-assignment framework.clarify the RACI
open dependency
A required preceding task or condition not yet satisfied.track open dependencies

Data preparation and import mapping have different owners. The customer must approve appropriate source data, while the supplier maps the agreed fields. Secure transfer arrangements, access, and any rollback procedures must follow the actual implementation plan.

C Notice the language

A shared plan needs explicit owners

Customers may interpret a friendly agreement with a target as a promise. State what is agreed, what is still unassigned, and which conditions govern launch. Confirm responsibility without making the customer feel that the supplier is transferring every difficulty to them.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Confirm the objective
Which workflow must the team be able to complete on launch day?
Qualify the date
Friday is the target, but readiness is not yet confirmed.
Name a dependency
The test import depends on an approved customer export.
Separate responsibilities
Your team prepares the data; our team maps the agreed fields.
Ask for an owner
Who can own the export and confirm its availability?
Check capacity
Has that person accepted the task and the timing?
Define acceptance
We need users to complete the agreed workflow successfully.
Clarify attendance
Who will attend the administrator training?
Avoid assuming completion
A calendar invitation does not mean the training has happened.
Set a review
Let us review readiness on Thursday at fifteen hundred UTC.
State a conditional sequence
Once the approved export is available, we can assess the import schedule.
Protect the data
Use the agreed secure transfer route and only the required fields.
Check support
What support coverage is agreed for the initial operating period?
Keep gaps visible
The export owner and training slot are still open.
Agree a decision rule
Confirm launch only after the agreed conditions are met.
Close with ownership
I will update the plan with each owner, dependency, and confirmed date.
6 language and precision notes
Target
Describes the desired date without silently guaranteeing it.
Depends on
Names the preceding condition rather than blaming another team.
Has accepted
Checks actual ownership, not merely a name on a chart.
Once
Makes the sequence conditional on a completed prerequisite.
Still open
Identifies unresolved work without declaring the entire project a failure.
Only after
Makes the readiness condition explicit.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which launch statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Friday remains a target pending the four agreed readiness conditions.

A desired date cannot substitute for the stated acceptance and preparation conditions.

2. Which ownership statement matches the brief?

Answer and explanation

D. Customer preparation and supplier mapping are separate responsibilities.

The allocation distinguishes source-data preparation from the supplier's mapping work.

3. What does a training invitation establish?

Answer and explanation

C. A proposed or scheduled event, not completed training

An invitation concerns scheduling and does not prove attendance, learning, or acceptance.

4. What belongs in the Thursday decision?

Answer and explanation

A. The actual status of each launch condition and any unresolved dependency

Each required condition must be checked. An average can conceal a blocking dependency; completed training or file delivery cannot replace testing and user acceptance.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Turn a hoped-for launch into a shared readiness plan

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Omar: Customer implementation lead / Lena: Customer success manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • open dependency
  • handover
  • target date
  • data preparation
  • user acceptance
  • test import
  • readiness review
  • success plan
  • data owner
  • training slot
  1. Omar
    I have a launch announcement ready for next Friday. Before I send it, can you confirm we will be ready to use the system?
  2. Lena
    Friday is still our 1, but I cannot confirm readiness yet. The export owner and administrator training slot are open. I would like us to settle those responsibilities before you announce a firm launch.
  3. Omar
    I thought migration was part of your service. Which part are you asking us to own? My team cannot take over your technical import work.
  4. Lena
    Let us separate 2 from our import work. Your team approves the source export; we map the agreed fields and run the import checks. We are not asking your team to perform those technical checks.
  5. Omar
    That distinction helps. I can coordinate the export, but I need our data lead to confirm when an approved file will be available.
  6. Lena
    Please name that person as the 3 in the plan and check their availability. We also need the agreed secure transfer route; please do not send the full customer database in an ordinary email.
  7. Omar
    Once the file is available, could the team start working immediately, or is there another check first?
  8. Lena
    We need a successful 4 before operational use. Then your users need to verify the main workflow against the agreed criteria. A file arriving is progress, but it does not complete those checks.
  9. Omar
    Two team leads could check the workflow. I will confirm their availability before putting their names in the plan.
  10. Lena
    Good. Their 5 needs a clear basis: the agreed task, expected result, and any exceptions. We should know what successful completion means before deciding whether the system is ready.
  11. Omar
    The training invitation is not out yet. Our administrator works across two sites, so I need to check which session they can attend.
  12. Lena
    I will coordinate the 6 with you. Sending an invitation is not the same as completing administrator training, which is one of our launch conditions.
  13. Omar
    I still need to tell the wider team something today. I do not want silence to create a different set of expectations.
  14. Lena
    Tell them Friday remains the target, with confirmation following the 7 on Thursday at fifteen hundred UTC. That gives them a real decision point without implying that unresolved work is already finished.
  15. Omar
    What if the export is late but training is complete? Would we still describe the project as ready?
  16. Lena
    No. The export would remain an 8. We must assess the effect on testing and acceptance rather than count one completed task as a substitute for another.
  17. Omar
    Please also make clear who supports the team after launch. They should not have to guess which contact to use.
  18. Lena
    We will confirm the 9 and initial support arrangements. Coverage and contacts need to be explicit, with any enhanced support period defined rather than assumed to continue indefinitely.
  19. Omar
    I will confirm the data lead and user testers, check their availability, and send a conditional update to the team.
  20. Lena
    I will update the 10 with the owners, dependencies, and actual status. At Thursday's review, we will decide from the evidence against all four conditions, not from the date alone.

Answers and explanations
  1. target date Turn 2. Target date identifies the intended timing, while the required launch conditions remain unverified.
  2. data preparation Turn 4. The customer prepares and approves source data; the supplier separately handles the agreed import mapping.
  3. data owner Turn 6. Naming a data owner identifies accountability, but availability and approval still require confirmation.
  4. test import Turn 8. The test import checks the migration before operational use; receiving a file does not establish success.
  5. user acceptance Turn 10. User acceptance checks the agreed workflow against customer needs, separately from technical import completion.
  6. training slot Turn 12. A training slot needs confirmed trainer and participant availability; an invitation alone does not complete training.
  7. readiness review Turn 14. The Thursday review assesses the four conditions; scheduling the review does not satisfy them.
  8. open dependency Turn 16. An unresolved export still blocks the dependent checks even if administrator training is complete.
  9. handover Turn 18. The handover transfers support responsibility and contact details, rather than assuming the project team remains available indefinitely.
  10. success plan Turn 20. The success plan brings outcomes, owners, dependencies, and evidence together for the readiness decision.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Omar: I have a launch announcement ready for next Friday. Before I send it, can you confirm we will be ready to use the system?
  2. Lena: Friday is still our target date, but I cannot confirm readiness yet. The export owner and administrator training slot are open. I would like us to settle those responsibilities before you announce a firm launch.
  3. Omar: I thought migration was part of your service. Which part are you asking us to own? My team cannot take over your technical import work.
  4. Lena: Let us separate data preparation from our import work. Your team approves the source export; we map the agreed fields and run the import checks. We are not asking your team to perform those technical checks.
  5. Omar: That distinction helps. I can coordinate the export, but I need our data lead to confirm when an approved file will be available.
  6. Lena: Please name that person as the data owner in the plan and check their availability. We also need the agreed secure transfer route; please do not send the full customer database in an ordinary email.
  7. Omar: Once the file is available, could the team start working immediately, or is there another check first?
  8. Lena: We need a successful test import before operational use. Then your users need to verify the main workflow against the agreed criteria. A file arriving is progress, but it does not complete those checks.
  9. Omar: Two team leads could check the workflow. I will confirm their availability before putting their names in the plan.
  10. Lena: Good. Their user acceptance needs a clear basis: the agreed task, expected result, and any exceptions. We should know what successful completion means before deciding whether the system is ready.
  11. Omar: The training invitation is not out yet. Our administrator works across two sites, so I need to check which session they can attend.
  12. Lena: I will coordinate the training slot with you. Sending an invitation is not the same as completing administrator training, which is one of our launch conditions.
  13. Omar: I still need to tell the wider team something today. I do not want silence to create a different set of expectations.
  14. Lena: Tell them Friday remains the target, with confirmation following the readiness review on Thursday at fifteen hundred UTC. That gives them a real decision point without implying that unresolved work is already finished.
  15. Omar: What if the export is late but training is complete? Would we still describe the project as ready?
  16. Lena: No. The export would remain an open dependency. We must assess the effect on testing and acceptance rather than count one completed task as a substitute for another.
  17. Omar: Please also make clear who supports the team after launch. They should not have to guess which contact to use.
  18. Lena: We will confirm the handover and initial support arrangements. Coverage and contacts need to be explicit, with any enhanced support period defined rather than assumed to continue indefinitely.
  19. Omar: I will confirm the data lead and user testers, check their availability, and send a conditional update to the team.
  20. Lena: I will update the success plan with the owners, dependencies, and actual status. At Thursday's review, we will decide from the evidence against all four conditions, not from the date alone.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Next Friday is an expectation, not a confirmed go-live

The lesson's customer expects go-live next Friday, while migration responsibilities and training dates remain unassigned.

  1. Customer success manager: You expect to go live next Friday. Could we clarify who owns migration and when training will take place?
  2. Customer project lead: Those responsibilities and dates haven't been assigned yet. I assumed implementation would handle them automatically.
  3. Customer success manager: We need explicit ownership before treating the date as confirmed. Which customer owner can coordinate the migration inputs?
  4. Customer project lead: I need to identify that person internally rather than assign the task to someone without checking.
  5. Customer success manager: Thank you. Training also needs agreed participants and timing; we shouldn't infer readiness from a target date.
  6. Customer project lead: Could we keep next Friday as an aspiration while making the outstanding dependencies visible?
  7. Customer success manager: Yes, clearly labeled as your target, not a confirmed go-live commitment from the implementation team.
  8. Customer project lead: I'll clarify the customer owner and bring available training times for discussion.
  9. Customer success manager: Then we'll review the onboarding dependencies together and assess a realistic plan using the confirmed information.
  10. Customer project lead: Agreed. I won't tell our users that launch is settled while those responsibilities remain open.
Imported data or validated data?

Separate fictional case: a customer's onboarding tracker says data ready after an upload; the customer has not reviewed the imported records.

  1. Implementation manager: The tracker says data ready. Does that mean the upload finished, or that your team validated the imported records?
  2. Customer data owner: Only the upload is complete. We haven't reviewed the records in the new system yet.
  3. Implementation manager: Then let's clarify the label so nobody treats transfer completion as readiness for live use.
  4. Customer data owner: What do you need us to confirm before you can assess the remaining implementation steps?
  5. Implementation manager: We need the agreed validation outcome and any unresolved issues, using the project's actual acceptance process.
  6. Customer data owner: I can coordinate that review, but I don't yet know how long it will take.
  7. Implementation manager: Please confirm timing after checking capacity. I won't invent a go-live date based on an unreviewed upload.
  8. Customer data owner: Understood. I'll change our spoken status to uploaded, validation pending when I brief the project lead.
  9. Implementation manager: Good. Once you report the review result, we can discuss its impact on the onboarding plan.
  10. Customer data owner: I'll return with that result or clearly identified gaps, rather than call everything ready prematurely.
Administrator training or training for everyone?

Separate fictional case: a fictional onboarding plan lists one training session, but the customer expects both administrator and end-user instruction; audience scope is unconfirmed.

  1. Customer enablement lead: The plan lists one training session. Could you clarify whether that covers administrators, end users, or both?
  2. Customer success manager: The audience isn't specified, so I need to check the agreed onboarding scope before confirming coverage.
  3. Customer enablement lead: We assumed everyone would attend the same session, but administrators have different setup responsibilities.
  4. Customer success manager: That distinction matters. One calendar entry doesn't establish that both audiences' needs have been planned.
  5. Customer enablement lead: Could we identify what each group needs to do before selecting the session format?
  6. Customer success manager: Yes. Then we can assess the requirements against the implementation arrangement and flag any additional scope.
  7. Customer enablement lead: I can provide the audience roles, but I shouldn't promise separate sessions until you've checked availability and scope.
  8. Customer success manager: Correct. I'll clarify the intended training deliverable and what remains unresolved.
  9. Customer enablement lead: Let's keep the training date provisional until the audience and purpose are agreed.
  10. Customer success manager: Agreed. We'll confirm those details before using training scheduled as evidence of go-live readiness.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Turn a hoped-for launch into a shared readiness plan
  1. Read Omar and Lena aloud, then swap roles. Explain customer data preparation and supplier mapping as separate, coordinated responsibilities.
  2. Check all ten gaps. Reread the four launch conditions and Thursday fifteen-hundred UTC review without substituting one completed task for another.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Confirm the conditions for another launch

A Monday launch remains a target. Customer lead Farah owns source-data approval. Supplier lead Ben owns import mapping. Training is complete, but the test import has failed. The readiness review is Friday at 10:00 UTC.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Customer: "Monday is still a ___."

Answer and explanation

C. target

The launch is not confirmed because a required import check has failed.

2. Supplier: "Source-data approval belongs to ___."

Answer and explanation

A. Farah

The brief assigns source-data approval to Farah, not the supplier mapping lead.

3. Customer: "The incomplete technical condition is the ___."

Answer and explanation

C. test import

Training is complete, but the test import has not succeeded.

4. Supplier: "The readiness review is Friday at ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 10:00 UTC

The supplied time and zone identify the scheduled review, not automatic launch approval.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Onboarding and Implementation Expectations
Original fictional case: Customer lead Omar expects a launch on Friday, 9 October. The export owner and training slot are still unassigned. Customer success manager Lena cannot confirm readiness. The agreed launch conditions are an approved customer export, a successful test import, user acceptance of the main workflow, and completed administrator training. The customer owns data preparation; the supplier owns import mapping and training delivery. No one has yet confirmed that the required people are available. A readiness review is scheduled for Thursday at 15:00 UTC.
Language workshop: Turn a hoped-for launch into a shared readiness plan
Communication goal: Clarify owners, dependencies, and acceptance checks before confirming a customer launch date.
Language guidance: A target date is not evidence of readiness. The four stated conditions are export approval, a successful test import, workflow acceptance, and administrator training. The Thursday review checks those conditions; holding the meeting alone does not satisfy them.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which workflow must the team be able to complete on launch day? / Friday is the target, but readiness is not yet confirmed. / The test import depends on an approved customer export. / Your team prepares the data; our team maps the agreed fields. / Who can own the export and confirm its availability? / Has that person accepted the task and the timing? / We need users to complete the agreed workflow successfully. / Who will attend the administrator training? / A calendar invitation does not mean the training has happened. / Let us review readiness on Thursday at fifteen hundred UTC. / Once the approved export is available, we can assess the import schedule. / Use the agreed secure transfer route and only the required fields. / What support coverage is agreed for the initial operating period? / The export owner and training slot are still open. / Confirm launch only after the agreed conditions are met. / I will update the plan with each owner, dependency, and confirmed date.
Vocabulary: onboarding: The process of preparing a customer to use a service effectively. / implementation: Work to configure, connect, and introduce a solution. / kickoff: The initial meeting aligning purpose, people, and approach. / success plan: A shared record of outcomes, actions, ownership, and measures. / target date: A desired date whose commitment status must be specified. / go-live: The start of operational use under agreed conditions. / dependency: A task or condition on which another step relies. / prerequisite: Something required before a particular activity can proceed. / data migration: Moving data between systems with appropriate checks. / source export: Data extracted from the originating system. / data mapping: Specifying how source fields correspond to destination fields. / data owner: The role accountable for the relevant data and its use. / test import: A controlled trial of bringing data into the destination. / UAT: User acceptance testing; users checking the solution against agreed needs. / acceptance criterion: A defined condition used to judge whether work is acceptable. / administrator: A user with specified management permissions in the service. / training slot: A scheduled period with the required trainer and participants. / readiness review: A check of whether the conditions for the next stage are met. / cutover: The coordinated transition into a new operational arrangement. / rollback plan: An approved approach for reversing a change if required. / hypercare: A defined period of enhanced support after introduction. / handover: Transfer of responsibility with the necessary information. / RACI: Responsible, accountable, consulted, and informed; a role-assignment framework. / open dependency: A required preceding task or condition not yet satisfied.
Speaking task: Read Omar and Lena aloud, then swap roles. Explain customer data preparation and supplier mapping as separate, coordinated responsibilities. Check all ten gaps. Reread the four launch conditions and Thursday fifteen-hundred UTC review without substituting one completed task for another. Repeat the conditional announcement and ownership close, retaining the unassigned people and training slot as open work.
Partner: Omar: Customer implementation lead / Lena: Customer success manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A Monday launch remains a target. Customer lead Farah owns source-data approval. Supplier lead Ben owns import mapping. Training is complete, but the test import has failed. The readiness review is Friday at 10:00 UTC.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Omar: Please also make clear who supports the team after launch. They should not have to guess which contact to use.
Lena: We will confirm the handover and initial support arrangements. Coverage and contacts need to be explicit, with any enhanced support period defined rather than assumed to continue indefinitely.
Omar: I will confirm the data lead and user testers, check their availability, and send a conditional update to the team.

END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

REFERENCE
Course: Customer Success English
Professional audience: customer success managers, account managers, onboarding specialists, support escalation leads, renewals managers, implementation teams, and post-sale revenue leaders
Scope: All accounts, products, people, metrics, policies, dates, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not technical, financial, legal, or security advice. Follow actual contracts, approved support guidance, data-handling rules, and authorized escalation procedures. Health-score definitions and renewal processes vary by organization.
Lesson 01: Onboarding and Implementation Expectations
Original fictional case: Customer lead Omar expects a launch on Friday, 9 October. The export owner and training slot are still unassigned. Customer success manager Lena cannot confirm readiness. The agreed launch conditions are an approved customer export, a successful test import, user acceptance of the main workflow, and completed administrator training. The customer owns data preparation; the supplier owns import mapping and training delivery. No one has yet confirmed that the required people are available. A readiness review is scheduled for Thursday at 15:00 UTC.
Language workshop: Turn a hoped-for launch into a shared readiness plan
Communication goal: Clarify owners, dependencies, and acceptance checks before confirming a customer launch date.
Language guidance: A target date is not evidence of readiness. The four stated conditions are export approval, a successful test import, workflow acceptance, and administrator training. The Thursday review checks those conditions; holding the meeting alone does not satisfy them.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which workflow must the team be able to complete on launch day? / Friday is the target, but readiness is not yet confirmed. / The test import depends on an approved customer export. / Your team prepares the data; our team maps the agreed fields. / Who can own the export and confirm its availability? / Has that person accepted the task and the timing? / We need users to complete the agreed workflow successfully. / Who will attend the administrator training? / A calendar invitation does not mean the training has happened. / Let us review readiness on Thursday at fifteen hundred UTC. / Once the approved export is available, we can assess the import schedule. / Use the agreed secure transfer route and only the required fields. / What support coverage is agreed for the initial operating period? / The export owner and training slot are still open. / Confirm launch only after the agreed conditions are met. / I will update the plan with each owner, dependency, and confirmed date.
Vocabulary: onboarding: The process of preparing a customer to use a service effectively. / implementation: Work to configure, connect, and introduce a solution. / kickoff: The initial meeting aligning purpose, people, and approach. / success plan: A shared record of outcomes, actions, ownership, and measures. / target date: A desired date whose commitment status must be specified. / go-live: The start of operational use under agreed conditions. / dependency: A task or condition on which another step relies. / prerequisite: Something required before a particular activity can proceed. / data migration: Moving data between systems with appropriate checks. / source export: Data extracted from the originating system. / data mapping: Specifying how source fields correspond to destination fields. / data owner: The role accountable for the relevant data and its use. / test import: A controlled trial of bringing data into the destination. / UAT: User acceptance testing; users checking the solution against agreed needs. / acceptance criterion: A defined condition used to judge whether work is acceptable. / administrator: A user with specified management permissions in the service. / training slot: A scheduled period with the required trainer and participants. / readiness review: A check of whether the conditions for the next stage are met. / cutover: The coordinated transition into a new operational arrangement. / rollback plan: An approved approach for reversing a change if required. / hypercare: A defined period of enhanced support after introduction. / handover: Transfer of responsibility with the necessary information. / RACI: Responsible, accountable, consulted, and informed; a role-assignment framework. / open dependency: A required preceding task or condition not yet satisfied.
Speaking task: Read Omar and Lena aloud, then swap roles. Explain customer data preparation and supplier mapping as separate, coordinated responsibilities. Check all ten gaps. Reread the four launch conditions and Thursday fifteen-hundred UTC review without substituting one completed task for another. Repeat the conditional announcement and ownership close, retaining the unassigned people and training slot as open work.
Partner: Omar: Customer implementation lead / Lena: Customer success manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A Monday launch remains a target. Customer lead Farah owns source-data approval. Supplier lead Ben owns import mapping. Training is complete, but the test import has failed. The readiness review is Friday at 10:00 UTC.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Omar: Please also make clear who supports the team after launch. They should not have to guess which contact to use.
Lena: We will confirm the handover and initial support arrangements. Coverage and contacts need to be explicit, with any enhanced support period defined rather than assumed to continue indefinitely.
Omar: I will confirm the data lead and user testers, check their availability, and send a conditional update to the team.
Lena: I will update the success plan with the owners, dependencies, and actual status. At Thursday's review, we will decide from the evidence against all four conditions, not from the date alone.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Adoption Metrics and Health ScoresThe green score hides a workflow gap+

Your goal

Explain usage measures with the right population and distinguish access from meaningful adoption.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

The green score hides a workflow gap

Account C14 has 120 licensed users. In the last 30 days, 100 distinct users logged in. Of the 80 users whose roles require the approval workflow, only 30 completed it at least once. The dashboard health score is green because its current rule emphasizes logins. Customer lead Mei reports that some staff still use email approvals. Customer success manager Daniel must explain the different denominators and investigate the workflow gap without assuming every inactive license is wasted or that the account will certainly cancel.

Mei: Customer operations lead / Daniel: Customer success manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many licensed users logged in during the period?

Answer and explanation

A. 100 of 120

The brief reports one hundred distinct logins among one hundred twenty licensed users.

2. Which population is relevant to workflow completion here?

Answer and explanation

C. The 80 users whose roles require that workflow

The stated eligible group is eighty users, including those who did not complete the workflow.

3. Why is the dashboard green?

Answer and explanation

D. Its current rule emphasizes logins.

The score reflects its configured rule, not proof of workflow adoption or renewal.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Login activity is 100 divided by 120, approximately 83.3%. Relevant workflow completion is 30 divided by 80, or 37.5%. Thirty divided by 120 is 25%, but that answers a different question about all licensed users.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
adoption
Use of a product or workflow in the intended working context.assess adoption
licensed user
A person covered by the relevant access entitlement.count licensed users
active user
A user meeting a defined activity condition within a stated period.define active users
unique user
A distinct person or identity counted once under the measurement rule.count unique users
eligible population
The people for whom a particular measure is relevant.define the eligible population
denominator
The base quantity used in a fraction or rate.state the denominator
login rate
The share of a defined population that logged in during a period.calculate the login rate
workflow completion
Finishing the defined sequence of work.measure workflow completion
breadth of adoption
How widely relevant use extends across users, teams, or functions.assess adoption breadth
depth of adoption
How extensively intended capabilities are used.assess adoption depth
frequency
How often a defined action occurs within a period.measure usage frequency
cohort
A defined group followed or compared on a shared basis.compare user cohorts
health score
A configured indicator combining selected account signals.interpret the health score
weighting
The relative influence assigned to a component in a calculation.review score weighting
leading indicator
A measure used as a possible signal of a later result.test leading indicators
lagging indicator
A measure of an outcome that has already occurred.distinguish lagging indicators
engagement
Participation or interaction under a stated definition.assess stakeholder engagement
sentiment
Expressed attitudes or feelings about the relationship or service.record customer sentiment
usage telemetry
Recorded data about product interactions.validate usage telemetry
instrumentation
The setup used to capture events and measurements.check instrumentation
event definition
The rule specifying what a recorded action represents.verify event definitions
usage gap
A difference between intended and observed use.investigate the usage gap
adoption barrier
A condition making intended use difficult or impractical.identify adoption barriers
vanity metric
A flattering measure with weak relevance to the decision at hand.question a vanity metric

A user who completed the workflow once has met this period metric, not necessarily established a habit. A green health score is a configured signal. Its accuracy and usefulness depend on definitions, data quality, and relevance to the intended customer outcomes.

C Notice the language

Challenge the measure without dismissing the team

A green dashboard can feel reassuring to one team and disconnected from daily work to another. Acknowledge what the measure genuinely shows, then examine the missing outcome. Ask about role, frequency, access, and obstacles before calling users resistant.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the signal
The login count shows broad access during this period.
Name the gap
It does not show that the approval workflow is being used.
Specify the period
These figures cover the last thirty days.
Specify the population
Eighty users have roles that require this workflow.
State the rate
Thirty of eighty eligible users completed it, which is thirty-seven point five percent.
Distinguish another rate
Thirty out of all one hundred twenty licenses would answer a different question.
Check the event
What exactly triggers a workflow-completed event?
Avoid double counting
Count distinct users, not every repeated login.
Ask about the obstacle
Where do people switch back to email?
Keep the cause open
Low completion does not tell us the reason by itself.
Check role fit
Some licensed users may not need this workflow.
Challenge the score carefully
Green reflects the current scoring rule, not every aspect of account health.
Test relevance
Does this measure connect to the outcome you bought the product for?
Agree a targeted intervention
Let us address the confirmed obstacle before scheduling generic retraining.
Measure again
Use the same population and period definition for the follow-up.
Close with evidence
Record the gap, the confirmed barrier, and the next measurement date.
6 language and precision notes
Shows
Restricts the claim to what the measure actually establishes.
Does not show
Separates an access event from a completed customer task.
Of eighty
Makes the relevant denominator audible.
By itself
Leaves the cause open pending investigation.
Current rule
Explains a dashboard label without presenting it as an objective verdict.
Same definition
Makes later comparisons more meaningful.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement correctly describes workflow use?

Answer and explanation

C. Thirty of eighty eligible users completed it at least once in thirty days.

The measure counts distinct eligible completers within the stated period, not login activity or habit.

2. What is the workflow completion percentage?

Answer and explanation

C. 37.5%

Thirty divided by eighty is 0.375, whereas the other bases or complement answer different questions.

3. What should happen before blaming resistance?

Answer and explanation

A. Verify event definitions and ask where the workflow breaks down.

Event checks and workflow evidence can distinguish configuration, role, and training barriers. Generic retraining, a convenient score change, or consulting only successful users does not diagnose the reported problem.

4. Which follow-up comparison is sound?

Answer and explanation

D. Compare the same defined eligible group and period, noting any population changes.

Consistent definitions and explicit population changes are necessary to interpret movement in the rate.

E Conversations

The green score hides a workflow gap

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mei: Customer operations lead / Daniel: Customer success manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • eligible population
  • Frequency
  • denominator
  • health score
  • weighting
  • usage gap
  • login rate
  • event definition
  • cohort
  • adoption barrier
  1. Mei
    The dashboard is green, but my supervisors are still chasing approvals by email. What is that green score actually telling us?
  2. Daniel
    The green 1 mainly reflects logins at the moment. It tells us something about access, but it does not establish that the approval workflow is replacing email.
  3. Mei
    One hundred people logged in. That sounds encouraging, but I do not know how it relates to the work we actually need.
  4. Daniel
    It is one hundred distinct users out of one hundred twenty licenses in thirty days. The 2 is about eighty-three point three percent. Repeated visits by the same person do not increase that user count.
  5. Mei
    Only eighty people have roles that require approvals. The other forty use different parts of the product.
  6. Daniel
    Then eighty is the relevant 3 for this workflow measure. Thirty of those eighty completed it at least once, which is thirty-seven point five percent.
  7. Mei
    Our meeting slide used thirty out of one hundred twenty and said twenty-five percent. Is that wrong, or are we measuring something different?
  8. Daniel
    The arithmetic is correct, but the 4 for approvals is eighty. Twenty-five percent describes completers as a share of all licenses; thirty-seven point five percent describes completion among those expected to use this workflow.
  9. Mei
    Before we act on thirty, I want to know what the system calls a completed approval. Could starting a request count?
  10. Daniel
    We should verify the 5. A started request and a completed workflow are different events. We need to confirm that the recorded signal matches the action we are discussing.
  11. Mei
    The supervisors say people return to email when they cannot identify the next approver. I do not yet know whether that is configuration or training.
  12. Daniel
    That is a useful 6 to investigate. Let us look at the affected roles and steps before prescribing another general training session or assuming people simply dislike change.
  13. Mei
    Could the score include this workflow information? It is more relevant to our purchase than how often someone opens the homepage.
  14. Daniel
    We can review the 7 and the signals with our team. The purpose is a more useful account picture, not a different color chosen to justify a conclusion we already want.
  15. Mei
    I also want to distinguish someone who tried it once from a team that relies on it throughout the month.
  16. Daniel
    Exactly. 8 and task context matter alongside the number of users. We should not turn at least once into a claim of regular, independent use.
  17. Mei
    Let us begin with the supervisor group that reported the approver problem, then compare the same group after it is addressed.
  18. Daniel
    That gives us a defined 9. We will record membership changes and keep the completion definition consistent, so a rise does not merely reflect a different population.
  19. Mei
    Please keep the renewal discussion separate for now. I am describing a workflow problem, not announcing that we intend to leave.
  20. Daniel
    Agreed. The 10 deserves action, but it does not prove cancellation. We will validate the measure, investigate the approver obstacle, and agree what improvement we should check next.

Answers and explanations
  1. health score Turn 2. The health score reflects a configured rule emphasizing logins, not verified achievement of every customer outcome.
  2. login rate Turn 4. The login rate uses one hundred distinct users over one hundred twenty licensed users, approximately 83.3%.
  3. denominator Turn 6. Eighty is the eligible workflow population; using all licenses would answer a different adoption question.
  4. eligible population Turn 8. The eligible population contains the users whose roles require this workflow, rather than every licensed person.
  5. event definition Turn 10. The event definition establishes what the recorded completion means; opening or starting a request may differ.
  6. adoption barrier Turn 12. An adoption barrier is the obstacle to intended use; the reported approver problem still needs investigation.
  7. weighting Turn 14. Weighting determines each component's influence; changing it should improve relevance rather than merely change the color.
  8. Frequency Turn 16. Frequency describes repeated use within a defined period; one completion does not establish habitual adoption.
  9. cohort Turn 18. A cohort supplies a consistent group for follow-up, with membership changes disclosed rather than hidden.
  10. usage gap Turn 20. The usage gap is a difference between expected and observed use, not proof of an inevitable cancellation.

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Many logins, little workflow adoption

The lesson's account has frequent logins but limited use of the workflow the product was purchased to improve.

  1. Account director: The account has many logins. Why doesn't that establish strong adoption of the product?
  2. Customer success analyst: Logins show access activity, but the purchased workflow is barely being used. Those measures answer different questions.
  3. Account director: Then usage volume and adoption of the intended process shouldn't be treated as interchangeable.
  4. Customer success analyst: Exactly. A health score can summarize signals, but it needs definitions that reflect the account's actual purpose.
  5. Account director: Could high stakeholder engagement still make the account healthy even when that workflow use is low?
  6. Customer success analyst: It may be relevant, but we need evidence of that engagement and a transparent interpretation of the signals.
  7. Account director: I'll avoid saying frequent logins prove the customer is achieving the benefit they bought us for.
  8. Customer success analyst: Good. Ask what users are doing and what prevents use of the intended workflow.
  9. Account director: Let's review those questions with the customer before turning the dashboard color into a renewal prediction.
  10. Customer success analyst: Agreed. We'll distinguish measured activity, meaningful adoption, and outcomes rather than compress them into one claim.
One power user does not represent the whole team

Separate fictional case: one user completes ninety of a team's hundred workflow runs; no information about the other users' readiness has been reviewed.

  1. Customer success manager: The team recorded one hundred workflow runs, but one power user completed ninety of them.
  2. Customer team lead: So the total shows activity, but it doesn't demonstrate adoption across the whole team.
  3. Customer success manager: Correct. A concentrated usage pattern differs from broad participation, even when the total looks strong.
  4. Customer team lead: Could the other users be inactive because they don't need the workflow as often?
  5. Customer success manager: Possibly. We haven't reviewed their roles or readiness, so low activity isn't automatically a problem.
  6. Customer team lead: Then we should compare expected use by role before deciding who needs additional support.
  7. Customer success manager: Exactly. Stakeholder engagement and user needs provide context that a single activity count cannot supply.
  8. Customer team lead: I'll ask the team about their responsibilities rather than tell everyone to increase usage for the dashboard.
  9. Customer success manager: Good. The health score should support understanding, not encourage activity unrelated to the customer's purpose.
  10. Customer team lead: Let's review the role-based expectations and then agree whether any adoption support is needed.
A green score based on stale engagement data

Separate fictional case: a customer health score remains green using an old sponsor-meeting record; the sponsor has since changed and current engagement is unknown.

  1. Customer success analyst: The health score uses an old sponsor-meeting record. Since the sponsor changed, current engagement is unknown.
  2. Account manager: Does green mean the account is healthy now, or that the recorded inputs previously met the scoring rules?
  3. Customer success analyst: The latter. A score reflects its inputs and definitions; it doesn't independently verify the current relationship.
  4. Account manager: Then we should distinguish an unchanged color from confirmed stakeholder engagement with the new sponsor.
  5. Customer success analyst: Exactly. We need a current check before relying on that signal in the account review.
  6. Account manager: Could I simply change the score to red because the sponsor is new?
  7. Customer success analyst: Don't invent an interpretation. Follow our scoring definition and record the missing or outdated input explicitly.
  8. Account manager: I'll request an introduction and clarify the new sponsor's priorities rather than assume dissatisfaction.
  9. Customer success analyst: Good. Then we can update the input using actual evidence of engagement.
  10. Account manager: I'll explain the score's limitation in the meantime so the team doesn't mistake historical data for current confirmation.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The green score hides a workflow gap
  1. Read Mei and Daniel aloud, then swap roles. Say the population and thirty-day period before each rate.
  2. Correct all ten gaps. Reread the 100/120 login rate, 30/80 workflow rate, and 30/120 all-license comparison.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Use the correct adoption denominator

In one month, 45 of 60 licensed users log in. Only 40 users need the scheduling workflow; 20 of those complete it. A dashboard based on logins does not establish scheduling adoption.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Customer: "The login rate is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 75%

Forty-five divided by sixty is 0.75, using all licensed users as the base.

2. Manager: "The eligible scheduling population is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 40

Forty users need the workflow, so they form the relevant completion denominator.

3. Customer: "The eligible completion rate is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 50%

Twenty divided by forty is one half, not twenty divided by all licenses.

4. Manager: "A login is not necessarily a ___."

Answer and explanation

B. workflow completion

Access and completion are different events and must not be reported as interchangeable outcomes.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Customer Success English
Professional audience: customer success managers, account managers, onboarding specialists, support escalation leads, renewals managers, implementation teams, and post-sale revenue leaders
Scope: All accounts, products, people, metrics, policies, dates, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not technical, financial, legal, or security advice. Follow actual contracts, approved support guidance, data-handling rules, and authorized escalation procedures. Health-score definitions and renewal processes vary by organization.
Lesson 02: Adoption Metrics and Health Scores
Original fictional case: Account C14 has 120 licensed users. In the last 30 days, 100 distinct users logged in. Of the 80 users whose roles require the approval workflow, only 30 completed it at least once. The dashboard health score is green because its current rule emphasizes logins. Customer lead Mei reports that some staff still use email approvals. Customer success manager Daniel must explain the different denominators and investigate the workflow gap without assuming every inactive license is wasted or that the account will certainly cancel.
Language workshop: The green score hides a workflow gap
Communication goal: Explain usage measures with the right population and distinguish access from meaningful adoption.
Language guidance: Login activity is 100 divided by 120, approximately 83.3%. Relevant workflow completion is 30 divided by 80, or 37.5%. Thirty divided by 120 is 25%, but that answers a different question about all licensed users.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The login count shows broad access during this period. / It does not show that the approval workflow is being used. / These figures cover the last thirty days. / Eighty users have roles that require this workflow. / Thirty of eighty eligible users completed it, which is thirty-seven point five percent. / Thirty out of all one hundred twenty licenses would answer a different question. / What exactly triggers a workflow-completed event? / Count distinct users, not every repeated login. / Where do people switch back to email? / Low completion does not tell us the reason by itself. / Some licensed users may not need this workflow. / Green reflects the current scoring rule, not every aspect of account health. / Does this measure connect to the outcome you bought the product for? / Let us address the confirmed obstacle before scheduling generic retraining. / Use the same population and period definition for the follow-up. / Record the gap, the confirmed barrier, and the next measurement date.
Vocabulary: adoption: Use of a product or workflow in the intended working context. / licensed user: A person covered by the relevant access entitlement. / active user: A user meeting a defined activity condition within a stated period. / unique user: A distinct person or identity counted once under the measurement rule. / eligible population: The people for whom a particular measure is relevant. / denominator: The base quantity used in a fraction or rate. / login rate: The share of a defined population that logged in during a period. / workflow completion: Finishing the defined sequence of work. / breadth of adoption: How widely relevant use extends across users, teams, or functions. / depth of adoption: How extensively intended capabilities are used. / frequency: How often a defined action occurs within a period. / cohort: A defined group followed or compared on a shared basis. / health score: A configured indicator combining selected account signals. / weighting: The relative influence assigned to a component in a calculation. / leading indicator: A measure used as a possible signal of a later result. / lagging indicator: A measure of an outcome that has already occurred. / engagement: Participation or interaction under a stated definition. / sentiment: Expressed attitudes or feelings about the relationship or service. / usage telemetry: Recorded data about product interactions. / instrumentation: The setup used to capture events and measurements. / event definition: The rule specifying what a recorded action represents. / usage gap: A difference between intended and observed use. / adoption barrier: A condition making intended use difficult or impractical. / vanity metric: A flattering measure with weak relevance to the decision at hand.
Speaking task: Read Mei and Daniel aloud, then swap roles. Say the population and thirty-day period before each rate. Correct all ten gaps. Reread the 100/120 login rate, 30/80 workflow rate, and 30/120 all-license comparison. Repeat the targeted follow-up for the approver obstacle, keeping occasional completion, regular adoption, and cancellation risk distinct.
Partner: Mei: Customer operations lead / Daniel: Customer success manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: In one month, 45 of 60 licensed users log in. Only 40 users need the scheduling workflow; 20 of those complete it. A dashboard based on logins does not establish scheduling adoption.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mei: Let us begin with the supervisor group that reported the approver problem, then compare the same group after it is addressed.
Daniel: That gives us a defined cohort. We will record membership changes and keep the completion definition consistent, so a rise does not merely reflect a different population.
Mei: Please keep the renewal discussion separate for now. I am describing a workflow problem, not announcing that we intend to leave.
Daniel: Agreed. The usage gap deserves action, but it does not prove cancellation. We will validate the measure, investigate the approver obstacle, and agree what improvement we should check next.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
