Lesson 05: Renewals and Churn Risk
Original fictional case: Account R31 has a $60,000 annual recurring subscription scheduled for renewal on 12 November, six weeks away. Its main contact has left. New operations contact Ella has not confirmed current priorities or who owns the renewal decision and budget. Earlier reports focused on reducing a service backlog, but the business is now reorganizing. Customer success manager Rafael has no cancellation notice and no renewal approval. Ella agrees to a discovery meeting on 8 October at 10:00 UTC. Actual notice terms and the purchasing route still need checking.
Language workshop: Rebuild the renewal conversation after the main contact leaves
Communication goal: Explore changed priorities, verify decision roles, and describe renewal risk without treating it as a verdict.
Language guidance: A contact leaving is a risk signal, not a cancellation. An agreed meeting is progress, not renewal approval. The $60,000 recurring amount does not include an invented expansion or establish recognized revenue. Verify dates and notice terms in the actual agreement.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand that responsibilities have changed since our last review. / I do not want to assume the previous priorities are still yours. / Earlier discussions focused on reducing the service backlog. / Which outcomes matter most during the reorganization? / The recorded renewal date is the twelfth of November. / We have neither a cancellation notice nor renewal approval. / Who now coordinates the renewal decision? / Who owns the relevant budget? / How would you prefer us to involve the other decision participants? / We need the current purchasing route and required steps. / Let us verify the actual notice provisions rather than assume them. / Which results can your team validate from the last period? / We should understand the issue before proposing a commercial change. / Let us use the eighth of October to confirm priorities and decision roles. / The outcome remains unconfirmed while those gaps are open. / Record each action, owner, evidence source, and next date.
Vocabulary: renewal: Continuation of an agreement or subscription under applicable terms. / renewal date: The date specified for the relevant renewal event. / renewal owner: The role coordinating or responsible for the renewal decision process. / budget owner: The person accountable for the relevant funding decision. / stakeholder change: A change in people involved in or influencing the relationship. / executive sponsor: A senior person supporting the initiative within the customer organization. / champion: An internal advocate who actively helps the initiative progress. / reorganization: A change in organizational structure, roles, or responsibilities. / churn risk: The possibility of losing customer business, with its basis specified. / logo churn: Loss of customer accounts, distinct from the amount of revenue lost. / revenue churn: Recurring revenue lost under a specified measurement definition. / contraction: A reduction in business from an existing customer. / retention: Continuation of customer business under a stated measure. / GRR: Gross revenue retention; retained starting recurring revenue excluding expansion on a defined basis. / NRR: Net revenue retention; retained starting recurring revenue including expansion on a defined basis. / ARR: Annual recurring revenue, measured under the organization's stated inclusion rules. / risk signal: Evidence that may indicate a potential problem, not a certain outcome. / save plan: A defined set of actions addressing an evidenced retention risk. / renewal forecast: An estimate of renewal outcomes based on current evidence. / notice provision: A contract term concerning required notices and their conditions. / procurement route: The customer's process for reviewing and completing a purchase. / value evidence: Information supporting an actual customer benefit. / success criterion: A condition used to judge achievement of a desired result. / renewal plan: A shared sequence of renewal actions, owners, and dates.
Speaking task: Read Rafael and Ella aloud, then swap roles. Invite current priorities without making the new contact inherit the former sponsor's views. Check the ten gaps. Reread the distinct operational, budget, procurement, and renewal-owner roles. Repeat the October eighth meeting plan and November twelfth renewal date, keeping both cancellation and renewal approval unconfirmed.
Partner: Ella: New customer operations contact / Rafael: Customer success manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Account T08 renews on 20 December. Its sponsor has moved roles. New contact Hugo agrees to a needs review on 4 November but has not confirmed the budget owner. No renewal or cancellation decision is recorded.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rafael: I will keep the forecast open while we establish those facts. A scheduled discussion should not be reported as a completed renewal.
Ella: Please make the renewal forecast reflect that distinction. We can make progress without pretending the commercial decision has already happened.
Rafael: For the eighth, I will bring the factual history and open questions. You will confirm which roles should attend and what priorities they can speak to.
Ella: Yes. Then we can build a renewal plan with the actual decision participants, owners, and dates. Keep the twelfth of November visible, but do not use it to invent approvals we have not given.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Expansion and Upsell EthicsCheck the existing capacity before selling more seats+

Your goal

Explore additional demand, verify license rights, and align expansion with readiness and customer benefit.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Check the existing capacity before selling more seats

Customer administrator Nora asks success manager Sam for 25 additional licenses for a new team. The account already holds 80 named-user licenses: 50 assigned and 30 unassigned. Of the 50 assigned users, 35 have completed onboarding. The new team needs the same proposed workflow, but its requirements and training availability are unconfirmed. Use of existing unassigned seats requires the customer license owner to confirm entitlement and allocation. No additional purchase or allocation has been approved. Sam must assess fit and readiness before presenting a justified commercial option.

Nora: Customer administrator / Sam: Customer success manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many existing seats are unassigned?

Answer and explanation

B. 30

Eighty total licenses minus fifty assigned leaves thirty unassigned seats.

2. How many assigned users have completed onboarding?

Answer and explanation

D. 35 of 50

The stated completion count is thirty-five among the fifty currently assigned users.

3. What must happen before using existing unassigned seats?

Answer and explanation

B. The customer license owner must confirm entitlement and allocation.

The brief makes license-owner confirmation a prerequisite without assuming a new purchase is necessary.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The account has 30 unassigned seats, but availability is not automatically permission to allocate them. If the license owner authorizes 25 of those seats for the new team, 5 would remain unassigned; no additional quantity is assumed necessary from the count alone.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
expansion
Growth in business with an existing customer.assess an expansion opportunity
upsell
A move to a higher-value quantity, tier, or offering.justify an upsell
cross-sell
Sale of a different, related offering to an existing customer.assess cross-sell fit
seat
A unit of user access under specified licensing terms.count available seats
named-user license
An access entitlement assigned to an identified individual under the agreement.verify named-user licenses
concurrent-user license
Access measured by simultaneous use under the agreement's rules.distinguish concurrent-user licensing
assigned seat
A license allocated to a specified user under the relevant rules.review assigned seats
unassigned seat
A license not currently allocated to a user.verify unassigned seats
license utilization
Use or allocation of licenses under a stated measurement definition.define license utilization
entitlement
The access or usage rights granted by the applicable agreement.confirm entitlement
allocation
Assignment of available resources or access to a purpose or user.approve license allocation
license owner
The customer role responsible for managing the relevant license rights.consult the license owner
onboarding completion
Completion of defined preparation steps for a user or group.verify onboarding completion
readiness gap
A missing condition needed for effective use.address readiness gaps
incremental demand
Additional need beyond an established baseline.establish incremental demand
fit assessment
Evaluation of whether an offering meets the relevant needs.conduct a fit assessment
commercial option
A proposed purchase arrangement with defined scope and terms.compare commercial options
order form
The document specifying the relevant purchase details and terms.review the order form
proration
Proportional charging for a partial period under stated rules.explain proration
co-termination
Aligning the end dates of subscriptions under agreed terms.discuss co-termination
true-up
Adjustment to align reported or licensed usage with contractual requirements.review true-up terms
shelfware
Informal term for purchased software that is little used or unused.investigate shelfware
expansion readiness
Evidence that added scope can be used effectively.assess expansion readiness
customer benefit
A relevant advantage for the customer, supported by evidence or clearly labeled assumptions.establish customer benefit

Onboarding completion is 35 of 50 assigned users, or 70%. That is not 70% of all 80 licenses, and it does not prove independent workflow use. Named-user rights must not be treated as permission to share one identity among several people.

C Notice the language

A useful commercial conversation can begin with restraint

Customers may ask for a product quantity as a shortcut to describing a business need. Clarify the need and existing capacity before assuming the right purchase. Explain remaining work honestly, and do not use an adoption problem merely as a reason to withhold a suitable option.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Explore the request
What work will the twenty-five new users need to complete?
Check current capacity
The account has thirty unassigned seats.
Qualify availability
We need the license owner to confirm that those seats can be allocated to this team.
Avoid an automatic sale
The request does not yet establish a need for twenty-five additional licenses.
Check the arithmetic
Allocating twenty-five existing seats would leave five unassigned.
Respect the terms
Allocation must follow the actual entitlement and approved process.
State the readiness gap
Fifteen currently assigned users have not completed onboarding.
Avoid a false equivalence
Training completion does not prove regular workflow use.
Ask about resources
Who will own onboarding for the new team?
Assess fit
Let us confirm their workflow and access requirements.
Keep alternatives open
A justified additional purchase remains an option if existing rights do not meet the need.
Separate two decisions
License allocation and readiness for use need separate confirmation.
Avoid pressure
I will not invent an expiry date to accelerate the purchase.
Explain an actual quote
Any proposal will show quantity, term, charges, and approval status.
Avoid credential sharing
Use the approved user-access arrangement rather than shared named-user identities.
Close with next steps
Verify entitlement, confirm the workflow, and agree the onboarding owner before recommending quantity.
6 language and precision notes
Does not yet
Leaves room for evidence that an additional purchase is genuinely needed.
Would leave
Makes the arithmetic conditional on authorized allocation.
Separate confirmation
Prevents licensing rights from being confused with practical readiness.
Remains an option
Avoids treating responsible assessment as a blanket refusal to sell.
Actual entitlement
Directs the decision to the governing rights, not a dashboard count alone.
Before recommending
Connects the commercial recommendation to established customer need.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response best fits the request?

Answer and explanation

C. Confirm needs and allocation rights before deciding whether additional licenses are necessary.

The relevant decision depends on entitlement, customer need, and readiness, not an automatic sale or blanket refusal.

2. If 25 existing unassigned seats are authorized for the new team, how many remain?

Answer and explanation

B. 5

Thirty unassigned seats minus twenty-five allocated seats leaves five.

3. What is the stated onboarding completion rate among assigned users?

Answer and explanation

A. 70%

Thirty-five divided by fifty equals seventy percent; dividing by all eighty licenses uses a different denominator.

4. Which proposal would be transparent?

Answer and explanation

D. A justified quantity with term, charges, allocation basis, and approval status

A transparent commercial option explains its basis and terms rather than concealing need, permissions, or status.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

Check the existing capacity before selling more seats

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nora: Customer administrator / Sam: Customer success manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • fit assessment
  • readiness gap
  • entitlement
  • unassigned seats
  • incremental demand
  • named-user licenses
  • allocation
  • commercial option
  • Expansion readiness
  • onboarding completion
  1. Nora
    Another team wants access. Can you send an order for twenty-five licenses? I want to get them started without delaying their work.
  2. Sam
    I can help assess that, but let us check the existing 1 first. Your account has eighty licenses, fifty assigned and thirty unassigned. We should understand whether the new team needs additional rights or an allocation of existing capacity.
  3. Nora
    They expect to use the same approval workflow. Does having thirty spare seats mean I can assign twenty-five immediately?
  4. Sam
    We need the license owner to confirm the 2 and allocation. The count shows potential capacity, but I should not interpret the agreement or approve a different team's access on your behalf.
  5. Nora
    I will involve our license owner. If those seats can be allocated, the remaining number would be five, correct?
  6. Sam
    Correct, subject to that approved 3. Twenty-five taken from thirty leaves five. It would not increase the total from eighty to one hundred five.
  7. Nora
    I also see that only thirty-five of our fifty assigned users have finished onboarding. I do not want the new group to repeat the same difficulties.
  8. Sam
    That 4 figure is seventy percent of assigned users. We should identify the unfinished steps and barriers, but we should not assume every person has the same problem or that training alone proves adoption.
  9. Nora
    The new team has not confirmed who will attend training. Their manager asked for access before discussing the practical schedule.
  10. Sam
    That is a 5 separate from license quantity. Who will own their preparation, and when can the relevant users practice the workflow they actually need?
  11. Nora
    I can ask the manager. Would you still be willing to quote more seats if our existing rights do not cover that team?
  12. Sam
    Certainly. We should establish 6 after checking the actual rights and requirements. A suitable additional purchase is a valid option; it just needs a customer basis rather than an assumption from the first request.
  13. Nora
    There is one difference in their workflow: an extra approval step. We have not checked whether our current configuration can handle it.
  14. Sam
    Then include that in the 7. More seats do not solve a workflow mismatch by themselves. We should check capability and configuration alongside the training plan.
  15. Nora
    Some teams have shared access in other systems. I assume that is not something we should improvise here.
  16. Sam
    Correct. These are 8. Follow the approved identity and access arrangement; do not use shared credentials as a shortcut around the actual license or security requirements.
  17. Nora
    Once we know the right quantity, I will need a clear explanation of the term and any partial-period charge.
  18. Sam
    Any 9 should show those details, including the applicable charging basis. I will not invent a discount deadline or imply a new order is approved before your purchasing process is complete.
  19. Nora
    Then the immediate decision is not buy twenty-five or buy nothing. It is establish what rights and preparation the team needs.
  20. Sam
    Exactly. 10 comes from those facts. Let us confirm the license owner, the workflow requirements, and the onboarding owner, then recommend the quantity and arrangement that actually serve the team.

Answers and explanations
  1. unassigned seats Turn 2. Thirty unassigned seats exist, but their allocation still depends on the customer's confirmed licensing rights and process.
  2. entitlement Turn 4. Entitlement defines permitted use under the agreement; a visible unassigned count alone does not establish permission.
  3. allocation Turn 6. Authorized allocation of twenty-five from thirty unassigned seats would leave five, without increasing total licenses.
  4. onboarding completion Turn 8. Thirty-five of fifty assigned users completed onboarding, leaving fifteen whose preparation is not complete.
  5. readiness gap Turn 10. Unconfirmed training attendance and ownership are readiness gaps even if enough valid licenses are available.
  6. incremental demand Turn 12. Incremental demand is the additional need beyond usable existing rights, not simply the number of newly interested users.
  7. fit assessment Turn 14. The fit assessment checks the new team's actual workflow and access needs before recommending a purchase or rollout.
  8. named-user licenses Turn 16. Named-user licenses apply to identified individuals under their terms and must not be treated as shared-login permission.
  9. commercial option Turn 18. A commercial option should state the justified quantity, term, charges, and status rather than conceal assumptions.
  10. Expansion readiness Turn 20. Expansion readiness combines usable rights, workflow fit, and practical preparation; it is not established by a purchase request alone.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Nora: Another team wants access. Can you send an order for twenty-five licenses? I want to get them started without delaying their work.
  2. Sam: I can help assess that, but let us check the existing unassigned seats first. Your account has eighty licenses, fifty assigned and thirty unassigned. We should understand whether the new team needs additional rights or an allocation of existing capacity.
  3. Nora: They expect to use the same approval workflow. Does having thirty spare seats mean I can assign twenty-five immediately?
  4. Sam: We need the license owner to confirm the entitlement and allocation. The count shows potential capacity, but I should not interpret the agreement or approve a different team's access on your behalf.
  5. Nora: I will involve our license owner. If those seats can be allocated, the remaining number would be five, correct?
  6. Sam: Correct, subject to that approved allocation. Twenty-five taken from thirty leaves five. It would not increase the total from eighty to one hundred five.
  7. Nora: I also see that only thirty-five of our fifty assigned users have finished onboarding. I do not want the new group to repeat the same difficulties.
  8. Sam: That onboarding completion figure is seventy percent of assigned users. We should identify the unfinished steps and barriers, but we should not assume every person has the same problem or that training alone proves adoption.
  9. Nora: The new team has not confirmed who will attend training. Their manager asked for access before discussing the practical schedule.
  10. Sam: That is a readiness gap separate from license quantity. Who will own their preparation, and when can the relevant users practice the workflow they actually need?
  11. Nora: I can ask the manager. Would you still be willing to quote more seats if our existing rights do not cover that team?
  12. Sam: Certainly. We should establish incremental demand after checking the actual rights and requirements. A suitable additional purchase is a valid option; it just needs a customer basis rather than an assumption from the first request.
  13. Nora: There is one difference in their workflow: an extra approval step. We have not checked whether our current configuration can handle it.
  14. Sam: Then include that in the fit assessment. More seats do not solve a workflow mismatch by themselves. We should check capability and configuration alongside the training plan.
  15. Nora: Some teams have shared access in other systems. I assume that is not something we should improvise here.
  16. Sam: Correct. These are named-user licenses. Follow the approved identity and access arrangement; do not use shared credentials as a shortcut around the actual license or security requirements.
  17. Nora: Once we know the right quantity, I will need a clear explanation of the term and any partial-period charge.
  18. Sam: Any commercial option should show those details, including the applicable charging basis. I will not invent a discount deadline or imply a new order is approved before your purchasing process is complete.
  19. Nora: Then the immediate decision is not buy twenty-five or buy nothing. It is establish what rights and preparation the team needs.
  20. Sam: Exactly. Expansion readiness comes from those facts. Let us confirm the license owner, the workflow requirements, and the onboarding owner, then recommend the quantity and arrangement that actually serve the team.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

More licenses or better use of existing capacity?

The lesson's customer considers more licenses while many current users have not completed onboarding.

  1. Customer account lead: We are considering more licenses. Could expansion help us get more value from the service?
  2. Customer success manager: Possibly, but many current users haven't completed onboarding. We should compare expansion with improving readiness first.
  3. Customer account lead: I don't want an upsell recommendation that assumes every new license will immediately produce useful activity.
  4. Customer success manager: Agreed. Both options need the same use-case and readiness criteria, including who will use the service and why.
  5. Customer account lead: Completing onboarding may improve use of existing capacity, while expansion could address a genuinely new requirement.
  6. Customer success manager: Exactly. We need to establish that requirement rather than infer it from interest in more licenses.
  7. Customer account lead: Please help us review the current users and proposed new group before quoting a broader rollout.
  8. Customer success manager: I will. We'll make the support effort and unresolved readiness questions visible for each option.
  9. Customer account lead: Then we can choose a useful next step without treating a purchase itself as evidence of adoption.
  10. Customer success manager: That is the aim: compare the actual needs and tradeoffs before recommending expansion.
A new department pilot or an account-wide add-on

Separate fictional case: a customer asks about an add-on for five departments, but only one department has described a concrete use case; requirements elsewhere are unknown.

  1. Customer transformation lead: Could we add the module for all five departments, or should we start with the one defined use case?
  2. Customer success director: Let's compare a bounded pilot with wider expansion. Only one department's requirement is currently clear.
  3. Customer transformation lead: The wider purchase might simplify administration, but it could include departments whose needs we haven't assessed.
  4. Customer success director: Correct. A pilot limits initial scope while leaving later readiness and fit to be reviewed.
  5. Customer transformation lead: Would you guarantee that a successful pilot means the module fits the other four departments?
  6. Customer success director: No. Their workflows may differ; we need evidence rather than transferring the first department's result automatically.
  7. Customer transformation lead: Then compare support needs, scope, and decision points for both options without assuming universal use.
  8. Customer success director: Agreed. I'll also keep any commercial terms subject to the appropriate proposal and review.
  9. Customer transformation lead: I'll ask the other department owners to clarify their requirements before we commit them to an expansion.
  10. Customer success director: That will give us a fairer basis for a recommendation than account-wide interest alone.
An advanced feature versus a simpler workflow change

Separate fictional case: a customer seeks faster approval handling; a paid advanced module is available, but a change to its existing workflow might help and neither option has been tested.

  1. Customer operations manager: Should we buy the advanced module to speed up approvals, or review our existing workflow first?
  2. Customer success consultant: We should compare both against the same need. Neither option has been tested in your process.
  3. Customer operations manager: The module adds capability, while a workflow change might use what we already have.
  4. Customer success consultant: Yes. But we shouldn't claim either will reduce approval time without understanding the current obstacle.
  5. Customer operations manager: Could you include implementation effort and training readiness, not just the additional subscription price?
  6. Customer success consultant: Certainly. Those affect whether an expansion is useful, even if the feature appears attractive.
  7. Customer operations manager: I would prefer a proportionate assessment before an upsell proposal becomes the default answer.
  8. Customer success consultant: Agreed. Let's clarify the bottleneck and examine what each route requires and leaves unresolved.
  9. Customer operations manager: I'll involve the approval owner so the comparison reflects the actual use case.
  10. Customer success consultant: Then we can recommend a next step based on fit and readiness rather than on purchasing more capability.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Check the existing capacity before selling more seats
  1. Read Nora and Sam aloud, then swap roles. Check the thirty unassigned seats before discussing twenty-five additional licenses.
  2. Correct the ten gaps. Reread the conditional five-seat remainder and 35/50 onboarding rate with the right denominators.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Assess another request for more licenses

An account has 100 licenses, with 70 assigned and 30 unassigned. A new team needs 20 users. Existing allocation rights need confirmation. Of the 70 assigned users, 49 have completed onboarding.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: "The unassigned count is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 30

One hundred total licenses minus seventy assigned leaves thirty unassigned.

2. Customer: "If twenty existing seats are authorized, ___ remain."

Answer and explanation

C. ten

Thirty minus twenty leaves ten, conditional on approved allocation.

3. Manager: "Onboarding completion among assigned users is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 70%

Forty-nine divided by seventy is seventy percent, using assigned users as the denominator.

4. Customer: "Before allocation, we must confirm ___."

Answer and explanation

A. entitlement

A spare count does not itself establish permitted use for the new team.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 06: Expansion and Upsell Ethics
Original fictional case: Customer administrator Nora asks success manager Sam for 25 additional licenses for a new team. The account already holds 80 named-user licenses: 50 assigned and 30 unassigned. Of the 50 assigned users, 35 have completed onboarding. The new team needs the same proposed workflow, but its requirements and training availability are unconfirmed. Use of existing unassigned seats requires the customer license owner to confirm entitlement and allocation. No additional purchase or allocation has been approved. Sam must assess fit and readiness before presenting a justified commercial option.
Language workshop: Check the existing capacity before selling more seats
Communication goal: Explore additional demand, verify license rights, and align expansion with readiness and customer benefit.
Language guidance: The account has 30 unassigned seats, but availability is not automatically permission to allocate them. If the license owner authorizes 25 of those seats for the new team, 5 would remain unassigned; no additional quantity is assumed necessary from the count alone.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What work will the twenty-five new users need to complete? / The account has thirty unassigned seats. / We need the license owner to confirm that those seats can be allocated to this team. / The request does not yet establish a need for twenty-five additional licenses. / Allocating twenty-five existing seats would leave five unassigned. / Allocation must follow the actual entitlement and approved process. / Fifteen currently assigned users have not completed onboarding. / Training completion does not prove regular workflow use. / Who will own onboarding for the new team? / Let us confirm their workflow and access requirements. / A justified additional purchase remains an option if existing rights do not meet the need. / License allocation and readiness for use need separate confirmation. / I will not invent an expiry date to accelerate the purchase. / Any proposal will show quantity, term, charges, and approval status. / Use the approved user-access arrangement rather than shared named-user identities. / Verify entitlement, confirm the workflow, and agree the onboarding owner before recommending quantity.
Vocabulary: expansion: Growth in business with an existing customer. / upsell: A move to a higher-value quantity, tier, or offering. / cross-sell: Sale of a different, related offering to an existing customer. / seat: A unit of user access under specified licensing terms. / named-user license: An access entitlement assigned to an identified individual under the agreement. / concurrent-user license: Access measured by simultaneous use under the agreement's rules. / assigned seat: A license allocated to a specified user under the relevant rules. / unassigned seat: A license not currently allocated to a user. / license utilization: Use or allocation of licenses under a stated measurement definition. / entitlement: The access or usage rights granted by the applicable agreement. / allocation: Assignment of available resources or access to a purpose or user. / license owner: The customer role responsible for managing the relevant license rights. / onboarding completion: Completion of defined preparation steps for a user or group. / readiness gap: A missing condition needed for effective use. / incremental demand: Additional need beyond an established baseline. / fit assessment: Evaluation of whether an offering meets the relevant needs. / commercial option: A proposed purchase arrangement with defined scope and terms. / order form: The document specifying the relevant purchase details and terms. / proration: Proportional charging for a partial period under stated rules. / co-termination: Aligning the end dates of subscriptions under agreed terms. / true-up: Adjustment to align reported or licensed usage with contractual requirements. / shelfware: Informal term for purchased software that is little used or unused. / expansion readiness: Evidence that added scope can be used effectively. / customer benefit: A relevant advantage for the customer, supported by evidence or clearly labeled assumptions.
Speaking task: Read Nora and Sam aloud, then swap roles. Check the thirty unassigned seats before discussing twenty-five additional licenses. Correct the ten gaps. Reread the conditional five-seat remainder and 35/50 onboarding rate with the right denominators. Repeat the next steps for entitlement, the extra approval step, and training ownership before recommending a justified purchase or allocation.
Partner: Nora: Customer administrator / Sam: Customer success manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An account has 100 licenses, with 70 assigned and 30 unassigned. A new team needs 20 users. Existing allocation rights need confirmation. Of the 70 assigned users, 49 have completed onboarding.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Nora: Once we know the right quantity, I will need a clear explanation of the term and any partial-period charge.
Sam: Any commercial option should show those details, including the applicable charging basis. I will not invent a discount deadline or imply a new order is approved before your purchasing process is complete.
Nora: Then the immediate decision is not buy twenty-five or buy nothing. It is establish what rights and preparation the team needs.
Sam: Exactly. Expansion readiness comes from those facts. Let us confirm the license owner, the workflow requirements, and the onboarding owner, then recommend the quantity and arrangement that actually serve the team.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Nora: Once we know the right quantity, I will need a clear explanation of the term and any partial-period charge.
Sam: Any commercial option should show those details, including the applicable charging basis. I will not invent a discount deadline or imply a new order is approved before your purchasing process is complete.
Nora: Then the immediate decision is not buy twenty-five or buy nothing. It is establish what rights and preparation the team needs.
Sam: Exactly. Expansion readiness comes from those facts. Let us confirm the license owner, the workflow requirements, and the onboarding owner, then recommend the quantity and arrangement that actually serve the team.
END REFERENCE
07Product Feedback and Feature RequestsDiscover the workflow behind the requested button+

Your goal

Turn a proposed feature into a precise problem, usable evidence, and a responsible product-team handoff.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Discover the workflow behind the requested button

Customer operations lead Nia requests a bulk-approval button. Twenty approvers handle about 200 requests weekly; around 40 need individual exception review. Staff report repeated navigation between similar requests. The customer requires a separate authorized decision record for each request. The current product offers a saved filtered view but no confirmed bulk-approval capability. Success manager Teo must clarify the repeated work and pass the need to product manager Arun. Logging the request does not assign delivery priority or a release date. Any interim approach must preserve the decision-record requirement.

Nia: Customer operations lead / Teo: Customer success manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What problem has the customer reported?

Answer and explanation

D. Repeated navigation between similar approval requests

The brief identifies repetitive navigation, while retaining separate authorized decisions remains a requirement.

2. What record must an interim approach preserve?

Answer and explanation

A. A separate authorized decision for each request

The customer explicitly requires an individual authorized decision record for every request.

3. What does logging the feature request establish?

Answer and explanation

A. That the need has been recorded for assessment

Recording feedback does not itself determine priority, feasibility, or release timing.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

About 200 requests and 40 exceptions suggest about 160 non-exception requests in that weekly pattern, not 160 automatically approvable records. Routine status does not remove the stated requirement for an authorized decision on each request.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
feature request
A proposal for a product capability or change.capture a feature request
problem statement
A clear description of the difficulty to be addressed.refine the problem statement
use case
A specific situation in which a capability would be used.document the use case
workflow
The sequence of activities used to complete a task.map the workflow
friction point
A step that creates avoidable difficulty or effort.identify friction points
bulk action
An operation applied to multiple selected items.assess bulk-action needs
exception handling
The treatment of cases outside the normal pattern.preserve exception handling
audit trail
A traceable record of actions and decisions.preserve the audit trail
authorization
Permission to perform a specified action.verify authorization
saved view
A reusable display configured with selected criteria.configure a saved view
filter
A rule selecting which items are shown or processed.validate the filter
interim approach
A temporary way of working pending a longer-term decision.assess an interim approach
user story
A concise expression of a user's need and intended value.refine a user story
acceptance criteria
Conditions used to judge whether a proposed solution meets the need.define acceptance criteria
reproduction steps
A sequence allowing others to observe reported behavior.provide reproduction steps
representative example
An example reflecting the relevant real-world pattern.select representative examples
frequency
How often the relevant situation or action occurs.quantify task frequency
impact statement
An explanation of how a problem affects the customer's work.verify the impact statement
product backlog
A maintained collection of potential product work.review the product backlog
prioritization
Deciding the relative order or importance of work.explain prioritization
feasibility
Whether a proposed approach can be achieved under relevant constraints.assess feasibility
roadmap
A communicated plan for product direction and anticipated work.qualify roadmap information
discovery
Investigation of needs, context, and possible solutions.support product discovery
feedback loop
A process that returns assessment or outcome information to the source.close the feedback loop

A saved filtered view may reduce navigation, but it is not a bulk-approval capability. A logged request, a backlog entry, a roadmap item, and a released feature are different statuses. Confirm the actual status and its limits before communicating availability.

C Notice the language

Respect the proposed solution while exploring the problem

A customer may name a feature because it is the clearest way to express frustration. Do not dismiss the idea as merely a request. Ask for the relevant sequence and obstacle, then confirm the problem in words the customer recognizes before discussing alternative approaches.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Respect the idea
I understand why a bulk-approval button sounds useful.
Explore the sequence
Walk me through the repeated steps between two similar requests.
Identify the obstacle
Is the main effort navigation, comparison, or the decision itself?
Quantify frequency
About how many requests follow this pattern each week?
Separate exceptions
Which cases still need individual investigation?
Preserve the requirement
Every request must retain its own authorized decision record.
Check the current option
The saved view may reduce navigation, but it is not bulk approval.
Avoid premature advice
We need to verify the filter and permissions before recommending that approach.
State the problem
Repeated navigation slows review of similar requests.
Define a useful result
Reduce repeated navigation while preserving individual decisions and exception visibility.
Request minimal evidence
An approved, de-identified example of the workflow is enough for the initial assessment.
Explain the handoff
I will send the problem, frequency, impact, constraints, and examples to Arun.
Keep status clear
Recorded for assessment does not mean scheduled for release.
Avoid a roadmap promise
I do not have an approved delivery date to share.
Return the response
I will bring the product team's assessment back to you.
Close accurately
Let us confirm that this summary captures your need before we submit it.
6 language and precision notes
Sounds useful
Respects the proposed solution without claiming it is the chosen design.
Main effort
Distinguishes navigation from the judgment the user still needs to make.
While preserving
States a constraint that a faster approach must continue to satisfy.
May reduce
Labels the interim option as something to assess, not a proven outcome.
Recorded for assessment
Names a real status without implying implementation.
Captures your need
Checks that the handoff represents the customer accurately.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which problem statement best represents the case?

Answer and explanation

A. Repeated navigation slows similar-request review while individual decisions must remain traceable.

The statement captures the reported effort and preserves the explicit decision-record constraint.

2. Which interim statement is responsible?

Answer and explanation

D. A saved view may help navigation, subject to verified filters and permissions.

The known capability is a view, not bulk approval or permission to remove required controls.

3. What should the product handoff include?

Answer and explanation

A. The workflow, frequency, impact, constraints, and representative approved examples

The handoff must capture the actual task and constraints. Popularity, a proposed design, or a selectively scoped estimate cannot replace evidence of the workflow and required individual decisions.

4. Which customer update correctly describes backlog status?

Answer and explanation

D. The request is recorded for assessment; priority and delivery timing are not confirmed.

Recorded work remains distinct from prioritization, implementation, testing, and actual release.

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nia: Customer operations lead / Teo: Customer success manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

  1. Nia
    Could we have a bulk-approval button? Our approvers keep opening similar requests one by one, then going back to the full list.
  2. Teo
    I understand the 1. Before I pass it to product, can we identify which repeated steps are taking the time: finding the next request, comparing details, or making the decision?
  3. Nia
    Navigation is the part people complain about most. Twenty approvers handle about two hundred requests a week, and around forty need closer exception review.
  4. Teo
    Then the 2 should name that repeated navigation. We should also preserve the distinction between similar requests and exceptions rather than assume all two hundred can be handled identically.
  5. Nia
    Yes. Even a routine request needs its own authorized decision. We cannot lose the record of who decided what.
  6. Teo
    That 3 is a required constraint, not an optional detail. About one hundred sixty non-exception requests does not mean one hundred sixty records can be approved without an individual decision.
  7. Nia
    We currently move back to the full list after each review. Could the product at least keep the relevant requests together?
  8. Teo
    There is a 4 that may help navigation. We need to check its filters and permissions for your workflow. It is not a bulk-approval function, and I do not want to describe it as one.
  9. Nia
    We can test whether the view helps, provided the exceptions stay visible and nobody gains permission they should not have.
  10. Teo
    Those are useful 5 for assessing an interim approach. We can check navigation, exception visibility, and the required individual decision record without removing the controls to make the demonstration look faster.
  11. Nia
    What does Arun need from us? I do not want my team to produce a long specification before anyone looks at the need.
  12. Teo
    A concise 6 is enough for the initial discussion: who performs the task, the repeated steps, the frequency, and the obstacle. An approved de-identified example will help; we do not need unnecessary customer records.
  13. Nia
    Please make the navigation problem clear in your note. We are trying to avoid repeated clicks, not asking you to remove the individual review.
  14. Teo
    I will include that in the 7. I will send the workflow, frequency, constraints, and examples to Arun, then confirm that the summary represents what you actually need.
  15. Nia
    Once the request is logged, does that mean it is in the next release? I need to explain the status internally.
  16. Teo
    No. A 8 entry records potential work. Product still needs to assess the need, alternatives, effort, and wider priorities. I do not have an approved release date to share.
  17. Nia
    Could another customer need move ahead of ours even if our request came first?
  18. Teo
    Yes. 9 considers more than submission order. I can explain the assessment status and the evidence we supplied, but I cannot promise a ranking before that decision is made.
  19. Nia
    Then keep us informed when the assessment changes, including if the team proposes a different way to solve the problem.
  20. Teo
    I will close the 10. We will test the bounded interim option, preserve your decision requirements, and return the product response without confusing an idea, a plan, and a released capability.

Answers and explanations
  1. feature request Turn 2. The proposed bulk-approval button is a feature request, not yet an agreed solution or delivery commitment.
  2. problem statement Turn 4. The reported problem is repeated navigation during review, while exception handling and individual decisions remain necessary.
  3. audit trail Turn 6. The audit trail must retain a separate authorized decision for each request, including routine requests.
  4. saved view Turn 8. A saved view can organize selected records, but it does not create or confirm a bulk-approval capability.
  5. acceptance criteria Turn 10. Acceptance criteria specify what a useful approach must achieve while preserving exception visibility and authorization boundaries.
  6. use case Turn 12. The use case describes the actual approver task, sequence, and context without requiring the customer to design the product.
  7. impact statement Turn 14. The impact statement explains the navigation burden without falsely claiming that the customer wants approval judgment removed.
  8. product backlog Turn 16. A backlog records potential work; entry does not establish delivery priority or a release commitment.
  9. Prioritization Turn 18. Prioritization considers relative value and constraints rather than guaranteeing delivery order from submission date alone.
  10. feedback loop Turn 20. Closing the feedback loop returns the assessment to the customer, including alternatives or unchanged status rather than silence.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The requested feature is not yet the requirement

Separate fictional case aligned with the lesson: a reporting coordinator requests red warning badges because they open each invoice row to check approval before a Friday export. Individual approvals must remain, and supported display alternatives have not been checked.

  1. Customer success manager: You requested red warning badges. Which problem would those badges solve before your Friday export?
  2. Customer reporting coordinator: I open each invoice row to check approval. I need to find unapproved items without repeating that navigation.
  3. Customer success manager: So the obstacle is seeing approval status in the list, not completing the export itself?
  4. Customer reporting coordinator: Correct. The export works. We still need every invoice approved individually before including it.
  5. Customer success manager: Would a clear status label meet the need, or is the red color itself essential?
  6. Customer reporting coordinator: A clear label could work. Red was my suggested solution, not a requirement.
  7. Customer success manager: Then I will record visible approval status as the requirement, with individual review preserved.
  8. Customer reporting coordinator: Yes. Can you check existing display options before sending a new feature request?
  9. Customer success manager: I will check supported options. Neither a workaround nor a roadmap commitment is confirmed yet.
  10. Customer reporting coordinator: Understood. Please confirm what is available, and keep any proposed feature timing explicitly open.
A bulk button request with an approval constraint

Separate fictional case: a customer requests bulk approval, but says each item currently requires a separate review; the intended boundary of the proposed shortcut is unclear.

  1. Product liaison: You requested a bulk approval button. Which part should become faster while the individual review requirement remains?
  2. Customer operations lead: We still need separate review. I may be asking to reduce repeated navigation rather than approve everything together.
  3. Product liaison: That's an important distinction. Could you describe the sequence after you finish reviewing one item?
  4. Customer operations lead: I can show the current workflow in a sanitized example. I don't want to remove our review responsibility.
  5. Product liaison: Then the feature request should identify the navigation obstacle instead of imply a change to your approval controls.
  6. Customer operations lead: Would that make it a different product-feedback item rather than simply a renamed bulk-approval request?
  7. Product liaison: It may change the requirement significantly. We'll clarify it before the product team assesses possible solutions.
  8. Customer operations lead: Please don't promise a roadmap date based on the initial button description.
  9. Product liaison: Agreed. I'll record the reviewed requirement and keep implementation choices and timing unconfirmed.
  10. Customer operations lead: I'll provide the example and confirm which review steps must remain separate.
An export request with an unknown recipient need

Separate fictional case: a customer asks for a new export format because an external reviewer cannot use the current file; the reviewer's required structure is unknown.

  1. Customer success specialist: Could you clarify what the external reviewer needs that the current export doesn't provide?
  2. Customer reporting owner: They said the file wasn't usable, but we haven't obtained the required structure or an example.
  3. Customer success specialist: Then we should clarify that before requesting a new format based on our guess.
  4. Customer reporting owner: Could the problem be a missing field rather than the file type itself?
  5. Customer success specialist: Possibly. We need the actual obstacle, including whether it concerns content, structure, or access.
  6. Customer reporting owner: I'll ask for a non-sensitive sample specification rather than forward our full reporting file unnecessarily.
  7. Customer success specialist: Good. That will help product assess the feature request and any existing supported alternative.
  8. Customer reporting owner: Should I tell the reviewer that your roadmap will include their preferred format?
  9. Customer success specialist: No. We can commit to reviewing the clarified feedback, not to building an unassessed feature.
  10. Customer reporting owner: Understood. I'll obtain the requirement and keep any solution or delivery date open.

Answer and explanation

B. saved queue view

A view organizes tickets but does not establish batch-closure functionality.

Answer and explanation

D. not scheduled

No confirmed delivery schedule is supplied, so logging interest cannot become a release promise.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand why a bulk-approval button sounds useful. / Walk me through the repeated steps between two similar requests. / Is the main effort navigation, comparison, or the decision itself? / About how many requests follow this pattern each week? / Which cases still need individual investigation? / Every request must retain its own authorized decision record. / The saved view may reduce navigation, but it is not bulk approval. / We need to verify the filter and permissions before recommending that approach. / Repeated navigation slows review of similar requests. / Reduce repeated navigation while preserving individual decisions and exception visibility. / An approved, de-identified example of the workflow is enough for the initial assessment. / I will send the problem, frequency, impact, constraints, and examples to Arun. / Recorded for assessment does not mean scheduled for release. / I do not have an approved delivery date to share. / I will bring the product team's assessment back to you. / Let us confirm that this summary captures your need before we submit it.
Vocabulary: feature request: A proposal for a product capability or change. / problem statement: A clear description of the difficulty to be addressed. / use case: A specific situation in which a capability would be used. / workflow: The sequence of activities used to complete a task. / friction point: A step that creates avoidable difficulty or effort. / bulk action: An operation applied to multiple selected items. / exception handling: The treatment of cases outside the normal pattern. / audit trail: A traceable record of actions and decisions. / authorization: Permission to perform a specified action. / saved view: A reusable display configured with selected criteria. / filter: A rule selecting which items are shown or processed. / interim approach: A temporary way of working pending a longer-term decision. / user story: A concise expression of a user's need and intended value. / acceptance criteria: Conditions used to judge whether a proposed solution meets the need. / reproduction steps: A sequence allowing others to observe reported behavior. / representative example: An example reflecting the relevant real-world pattern. / frequency: How often the relevant situation or action occurs. / impact statement: An explanation of how a problem affects the customer's work. / product backlog: A maintained collection of potential product work. / prioritization: Deciding the relative order or importance of work. / feasibility: Whether a proposed approach can be achieved under relevant constraints. / roadmap: A communicated plan for product direction and anticipated work. / discovery: Investigation of needs, context, and possible solutions. / feedback loop: A process that returns assessment or outcome information to the source.
Speaking task: Read Nia and Teo aloud, then swap roles. Separate the requested button from the repeated-navigation problem. Check all ten gaps. Reread the forty exceptions and the requirement for an authorized decision on every request. Repeat the product handoff and interim saved-view proposal without promising bulk approval, backlog priority, or a release date.
Partner: Nia: Customer operations lead / Teo: Customer success manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A customer requests one-click batch closure for service tickets. The reported problem is repeated navigation. Each ticket must retain an authorized closure record. A saved queue view exists, but batch closure has not been confirmed or scheduled.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Nia: Could another customer need move ahead of ours even if our request came first?
Teo: Yes. Prioritization considers more than submission order. I can explain the assessment status and the evidence we supplied, but I cannot promise a ranking before that decision is made.
Nia: Then keep us informed when the assessment changes, including if the team proposes a different way to solve the problem.
Teo: I will close the feedback loop. We will test the bounded interim option, preserve your decision requirements, and return the product response without confusing an idea, a plan, and a released capability.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Professional audience: customer success managers, account managers, onboarding specialists, support escalation leads, renewals managers, implementation teams, and post-sale revenue leaders

Follow actual contracts, approved support guidance, data-handling rules, and authorized escalation procedures. Health-score definitions and renewal processes vary by organization.

08Difficult Customers and Boundary SettingRepair the expectation without promising a date you cannot confirm+

Your goal

Acknowledge impact, reconcile disputed records, and set a respectful boundary while keeping support available.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Repair the expectation without promising a date you cannot confirm

Customer lead Beth says an export feature was promised for October and demands confirmation of 31 October. The available 17 September note says the team is targeting late October, subject to testing; it does not record a confirmed release date. Success manager Idris must check any additional evidence rather than deny that other conversations occurred. He can arrange a manager review and provide a status update by 15:00 UTC on Thursday. He cannot approve compensation or promise delivery. Company procedure allows a pause after a clear boundary if personal insults continue, while preserving an appropriate support route.

Beth: Customer team lead / Idris: Customer success manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the available 17 September note say?

Answer and explanation

A. Late October is a target subject to testing.

The note records conditional planning language, not the confirmed date the customer requests.

2. What can Idris commit to?

Answer and explanation

A. A status update by Thursday at 15:00 UTC

Idris controls the stated communication step, while delivery, compensation, and the full history remain unresolved.

3. What should happen if other evidence exists?

Answer and explanation

D. Preserve and review it through the appropriate process.

Additional records may matter and should be reviewed without changing history or assuming new authority.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Targeting late October, subject to testing is conditional wording. It does not establish the requested 31 October commitment in the available note. That distinction does not prove that no other conversation or document exists, or determine any contractual remedy.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
expectation gap
A difference between what someone expected and what is currently understood or available.address the expectation gap
conditional estimate
A forecast dependent on stated assumptions or events.explain a conditional estimate
confirmed commitment
An undertaking established through the relevant authorized process.verify a confirmed commitment
qualification
Wording that limits or conditions a statement.preserve the qualification
delivery status
The current state of work toward making a capability available.verify delivery status
disputed record
Information whose meaning, completeness, or accuracy is challenged.reconcile a disputed record
contemporaneous note
A note made at or near the time of the relevant event.preserve contemporaneous notes
supporting evidence
Information that helps assess a claim or account.request supporting evidence
acknowledgment
Recognition of a concern or impact without necessarily accepting every claim.give a clear acknowledgment
service recovery
Work to address a service problem and rebuild trust.coordinate service recovery
complaint escalation
Routing a complaint to the appropriate reviewer or authority.arrange complaint escalation
review owner
The person responsible for coordinating an assessment.identify the review owner
compensation request
A request for money, credit, or another remedy.route a compensation request
commercial authority
Permission to make specified business decisions or commitments.confirm commercial authority
resolution path
The route for assessing and addressing an issue.explain the resolution path
boundary
A stated limit on conduct or participation.set a respectful boundary
personal insult
A demeaning remark directed at a person rather than the work or issue.address personal insults
de-escalation
Communication aimed at reducing tension while addressing the issue.use de-escalation language
pause
A temporary interruption with its purpose and next route clarified.explain a call pause
continuity of support
Maintaining an appropriate way to receive assistance.preserve continuity of support
factual chronology
A time-ordered account of events and evidence.compile a factual chronology
read-back
Repeating key points so another person can confirm or correct them.give a closing read-back
follow-through
Completing and communicating agreed actions.demonstrate follow-through
trust repair
Rebuilding confidence through accurate communication and reliable action.support trust repair

Acknowledging disruption does not require agreeing to an unverified date or compensation. A conduct boundary addresses behavior, not the legitimacy of the complaint. If a pause is needed under the stated procedure, explain the continuing support route and next contact.

C Notice the language

Firm dissatisfaction is not automatically abuse

A customer can dispute the record, demand accountability, or speak directly without crossing a conduct boundary. Respond to the substance first. If comments become personal, name the behavior calmly and offer a way to continue the work; do not use a boundary to avoid a legitimate complaint.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the effect
I understand that your team planned around an October expectation.
Invite the evidence
Please share the message or meeting record you relied on.
State the available wording
The note says late October, subject to testing.
Keep the record qualified
I have not yet reviewed every relevant communication.
Avoid a blanket denial
I am not saying that no other conversation occurred.
Separate the date
I cannot confirm the thirty-first of October from the information available.
Take ownership of review
I will arrange the manager review and preserve the records you provide.
Set the next update
I will update you by Thursday at fifteen hundred UTC.
Avoid promising the result
That update may report that the review is still pending.
Route the remedy request
I can record your compensation request, but I cannot approve it.
Accept direct criticism
You can challenge our handling of the issue.
Name the conduct boundary
I want to continue helping; please keep comments focused on the issue rather than personal insults.
Explain a possible pause
If the personal insults continue, we will pause this call and use the agreed support route.
Keep support available
The complaint will remain open through that route.
Confirm the read-back
Your concern is the missed expectation, the operational impact, and the requested review.
Close with follow-through
I will send the factual summary, the review owner, and the promised update time.
6 language and precision notes
Planned around
Acknowledges reliance and impact without deciding an unreviewed legal claim.
Available
Limits the statement to the information currently checked.
Not saying
Corrects an overly broad interpretation without retreating from the verified wording.
May report pending
Prevents the update time from becoming an implied resolution deadline.
Focused on the issue
Defines a behavior boundary while leaving criticism and complaint available.
Remain open
Preserves the route for legitimate review after a pause.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response addresses the disputed promise accurately?

Answer and explanation

A. The available note is conditional; let us review any other records you relied on.

The response distinguishes the available wording from the incomplete history without inventing certainty in either direction.

2. Which statement preserves a useful communication commitment?

Answer and explanation

C. I will update you by Thursday at 15:00 UTC, even if review is still pending.

Idris can commit to communication without promising a delivery or compensation outcome outside his authority.

3. Which behavior alone does not justify treating the customer as abusive?

Answer and explanation

D. Firmly disputing the record and requesting accountability

Direct criticism and a substantive complaint are not equivalent to personal abuse.

4. If the stated procedure requires a pause, what should Idris preserve?

Answer and explanation

A. A clear support route, complaint record, owner, and next contact

A conduct pause should not remove appropriate support or prevent review of the underlying concern.
