Dialogue 04: Quarterly business review
Original fictional setting: An account team prepares an outcome-focused review.
Professional roles: Account manager / Customer success manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Account manager: Our deck has twenty slides of usage charts. What business outcome are we discussing?
Customer success manager: The customer's goal was faster case handling. We should compare that measure with the agreed baseline.
Account manager: Do we have comparable data and the customer's confirmation of the definition?
Customer success manager: I'll verify both and show any gaps openly.
Account manager: Then end with the decisions and actions needed for the next quarter.
Customer success manager: Agreed. Product activity is useful context, but it doesn't automatically establish customer value.
Vocabulary in this exchange: usage: Observed use of a product or service under a defined measurement. / business outcome: A result the organization wants to achieve through its work.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 05: Renewal risk
Original fictional setting: An account team reviews a late renewal.
Professional roles: Renewals manager / Customer success manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Renewals manager: The renewal date is close, but procurement hasn't responded. Should I mark the account as lost?
Customer success manager: Not from silence alone. The sponsor says a budget review is underway.
Renewals manager: What is the confirmed buying process and decision date?
Customer success manager: I'll ask the sponsor and identify the procurement contact, without treating interest as approval.
Renewals manager: I'll keep the forecast status aligned with the evidence.
Customer success manager: We'll record the unresolved risks and the next agreed customer contact.
Vocabulary in this exchange: renewal: Continuation of a policy, contract, or subscription for a further period.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 06: Expansion fit
Original fictional setting: A sales colleague proposes an upgrade to a struggling customer.
Professional roles: Expansion manager / Customer success manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Expansion manager: The premium plan could solve their reporting request. Shall I send an upgrade proposal?
Customer success manager: First confirm the need and whether the current plan already supports an acceptable solution.
Expansion manager: They also haven't completed the original onboarding.
Customer success manager: Then adoption barriers deserve attention before we imply that more spend is the answer.
Expansion manager: I'll ask the solutions team to compare the actual options.
Customer success manager: We'll explain the fit, limits, and costs clearly and let the customer assess them against their priorities.
Vocabulary in this exchange: onboarding: The process of helping a new employee or customer begin effectively. / adoption: The extent to which people begin and continue using a product or process.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 07: Feature request handoff

END REFERENCE

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Dialogue 07: Feature request handoff
Original fictional setting: Customer success passes a customer request to product.
Professional roles: Customer success manager / Product manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Customer success manager: Three customers asked for a bulk approval button. I've attached examples of their current workflow.
Product manager: What task is slow, how often does it occur, and what constraints affect approval?
Customer success manager: They review hundreds of records, but different records may require different authority.
Product manager: That means a simple bulk action may introduce errors. Let's investigate the underlying need.
Customer success manager: I'll arrange discovery calls and avoid promising a delivery date.
Product manager: I'll record the evidence and keep you informed about the prioritization decision.
END REFERENCE
Dialogue 08: Resetting communication expectations
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Dialogue 08: Resetting communication expectations
Original fictional setting: An account team addresses repeated requests outside service hours.
Professional roles: Customer success manager / Support manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Customer success manager: The customer expects immediate replies from my personal phone every weekend.
Support manager: Explain the agreed support channels and escalation process, and acknowledge any earlier wording that created confusion.
Customer success manager: I'll distinguish urgent service incidents from routine account questions.
Support manager: Make sure the approved channel is usable and the actual coverage is clear.
Customer success manager: I'll offer a call to agree on a practical communication plan.
Support manager: I'll join to confirm support arrangements and follow up on any gap in the contracted service.
Vocabulary in this exchange: escalation: Raising an issue to a higher authority or different function because risk, urgency, or decision rights require it.
END REFERENCE

These settings adjust the practice; they are not an assessment of your level.

Read your ready-to-use prompt 
Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions after the REFERENCE block. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Customer Success English
Professional audience: customer success managers, account managers, onboarding specialists, support escalation leads, renewals managers, implementation teams, and post-sale revenue leaders
Scope: All accounts, products, people, metrics, policies, dates, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not technical, financial, legal, or security advice. Follow actual contracts, approved support guidance, data-handling rules, and authorized escalation procedures. Health-score definitions and renewal processes vary by organization.
Lesson 01: Onboarding and Implementation Expectations
Original fictional case: Customer lead Omar expects a launch on Friday, 9 October. The export owner and training slot are still unassigned. Customer success manager Lena cannot confirm readiness. The agreed launch conditions are an approved customer export, a successful test import, user acceptance of the main workflow, and completed administrator training. The customer owns data preparation; the supplier owns import mapping and training delivery. No one has yet confirmed that the required people are available. A readiness review is scheduled for Thursday at 15:00 UTC.
Language workshop: Turn a hoped-for launch into a shared readiness plan
Communication goal: Clarify owners, dependencies, and acceptance checks before confirming a customer launch date.
Language guidance: A target date is not evidence of readiness. The four stated conditions are export approval, a successful test import, workflow acceptance, and administrator training. The Thursday review checks those conditions; holding the meeting alone does not satisfy them.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which workflow must the team be able to complete on launch day? / Friday is the target, but readiness is not yet confirmed. / The test import depends on an approved customer export. / Your team prepares the data; our team maps the agreed fields. / Who can own the export and confirm its availability? / Has that person accepted the task and the timing? / We need users to complete the agreed workflow successfully. / Who will attend the administrator training? / A calendar invitation does not mean the training has happened. / Let us review readiness on Thursday at fifteen hundred UTC. / Once the approved export is available, we can assess the import schedule. / Use the agreed secure transfer route and only the required fields. / What support coverage is agreed for the initial operating period? / The export owner and training slot are still open. / Confirm launch only after the agreed conditions are met. / I will update the plan with each owner, dependency, and confirmed date.
Vocabulary: onboarding: The process of preparing a customer to use a service effectively. / implementation: Work to configure, connect, and introduce a solution. / kickoff: The initial meeting aligning purpose, people, and approach. / success plan: A shared record of outcomes, actions, ownership, and measures. / target date: A desired date whose commitment status must be specified. / go-live: The start of operational use under agreed conditions. / dependency: A task or condition on which another step relies. / prerequisite: Something required before a particular activity can proceed. / data migration: Moving data between systems with appropriate checks. / source export: Data extracted from the originating system. / data mapping: Specifying how source fields correspond to destination fields. / data owner: The role accountable for the relevant data and its use. / test import: A controlled trial of bringing data into the destination. / UAT: User acceptance testing; users checking the solution against agreed needs. / acceptance criterion: A defined condition used to judge whether work is acceptable. / administrator: A user with specified management permissions in the service. / training slot: A scheduled period with the required trainer and participants. / readiness review: A check of whether the conditions for the next stage are met. / cutover: The coordinated transition into a new operational arrangement. / rollback plan: An approved approach for reversing a change if required. / hypercare: A defined period of enhanced support after introduction. / handover: Transfer of responsibility with the necessary information. / RACI: Responsible, accountable, consulted, and informed; a role-assignment framework. / open dependency: A required preceding task or condition not yet satisfied.
Speaking task: Read Omar and Lena aloud, then swap roles. Explain customer data preparation and supplier mapping as separate, coordinated responsibilities. Check all ten gaps. Reread the four launch conditions and Thursday fifteen-hundred UTC review without substituting one completed task for another. Repeat the conditional announcement and ownership close, retaining the unassigned people and training slot as open work.
Partner: Omar: Customer implementation lead / Lena: Customer success manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A Monday launch remains a target. Customer lead Farah owns source-data approval. Supplier lead Ben owns import mapping. Training is complete, but the test import has failed. The readiness review is Friday at 10:00 UTC.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Omar: Please also make clear who supports the team after launch. They should not have to guess which contact to use.
Lena: We will confirm the handover and initial support arrangements. Coverage and contacts need to be explicit, with any enhanced support period defined rather than assumed to continue indefinitely.
Omar: I will confirm the data lead and user testers, check their availability, and send a conditional update to the team.
Lena: I will update the success plan with the owners, dependencies, and actual status. At Thursday's review, we will decide from the evidence against all four conditions, not from the date alone.
END REFERENCE

AI responses can contain mistakes. Compare feedback with the lesson and check specialist claims against the course references. Use these exercises for language practice; follow qualified guidance for actual professional decisions.

For teachers and study partners

Ask learners to respond before revealing the model. Give feedback on one meaning issue and one language pattern, then let them repeat. For mixed levels, offer the frames first and remove them in the second round.
